### 404. [Strict unimodality of the exact time-domain diffuse-light response under a Robin boundary condition](https://www.semanticscholar.org/paper/fa205de706a29489f2eff7ab13640a3577570060)

**Authors:** Junyong Eom, Jinwan Park  
**Date:** 2026-10-15  
**arXiv:** `2610.21431`  
**Source(s):** Semantic Scholar  
**Keyword score:** 6  
**Matched:** fluorescence imaging  

**Abstract**

Time-domain boundary measurements of diffuse light are temporal point-spread functions whose peak position is used as a robust scalar datum in diffuse optical and fluorescence imaging. We consider the exact response generated by a single fluorescent point target in a homogeneous half-space with a Robin boundary condition. The response is a temporal convolution of two diffusion Green-function factors containing a complementary-error-function boundary correction. For every positive diffusion, absorption, depth, and source--target and detector--target distance parameter, and for every nonnegative Robin parameter, we prove that the normalized exact response is a generalized gamma convolution density. Its extension by zero to the negative half-line is bell-shaped. A differentiable small-time expansion, established by sectorial analyticity and Cauchy estimates, together with the sign-change structure of the first two derivatives, excludes tangential critical points and yields a unique peak with strict increase before it and strict decrease after it. The argument is exact and requires no large-distance, source--detector balance, symmetry, restricted-time, or small-parameter assumption. The same conclusion holds after convolution with an exponential fluorescence-lifetime density for every positive lifetime. The key ingredients are an explicit Stieltjes representation of the Robin factor, hyperbolic complete monotonicity, closure of the generalized gamma convolution class, and bell-shape theory. A derivation of the exact half-space model and the differentiated endpoint asymptotics are included in two appendices.

**Abstract**

Reconciling efficient bacterial eradication with tissue safety remains a challenge in photothermal therapy (PTT) because of open‐loop thermal actuation. Herein, we propose a detachable bilayer hydrogel dressing comprising a poly(N‐isopropylacrylamide) (PNIPAM)‐based protective upper layer as a self‐regulating thermal cap, and a Ti 3 C 2 T x MXene/ciprofloxacin (CIP) therapeutic lower layer providing synergistic antibacterial therapy. Sequential replacement of the protection layer enables programmed temperature caps of 45°C for early infection control and 41°C for subsequent repair. Synergistic MXene/CIP effects achieve rapid and effective antibacterial activity (>99%) against both Escherichia coli and Staphylococcus aureus . In an infected rat wound model, the programmable temperature‐capped dressing shortens high‐temperature exposure, attenuates TNF‐α/IL‐6/p‐NF‐κB p65 expression, modulates VEGF/CD31‐associated angiogenic remodeling, and accelerates closure compared with controls. Furthermore, the percolated MXene network endows the therapeutic layer with stable negative temperature coefficient (NTC) behavior. To explore this extended electronic utility, we demonstrated the dressing's capability for in situ temperature monitoring. Additionally, a benchtop closed‐loop laser control system was constructed to serve as a proof‐of‐concept for future autonomous feedback regulation. Overall, this bioelectronic hybrid platform advances PTT from conventional open‐loop heating toward a thermally capped and feedback‐compatible therapeutic strategy, offering a versatile paradigm for safer infected wound management.

**Abstract**

Mixed ionic–electronic conductors (MIECs) are highly desirable for a wide range of electronic applications, however, developing single-phase MIECs with intrinsic transport properties under ambient conditions remains challenging. Herein, we report a Na+-ion and electron mixed-conducting metal–organic framework (MOF), Ni-HITP-SO3Na, by tethering anionic sulfonate side chains with Na+ counterions onto the 2,3,6,7,10,11-hexaiminotriphenylene (HITP) ligand of a d-π conjugated nickel bis(diimine)-based framework. Ni-HITP-SO3Na is highly crystalline with honeycomb networks and microporous with surface area of 412 m2 g–1. Remarkably, Ni-HITP-SO3Na exhibits dual transport without relying on conductive fillers, guest electrolytes, or external ion sources. Four-probe measurements reveal a high electronic conductivity of 0.7 ± 0.3 S cm–1, and DC polarization yields a sodium ionic conductivity of (3.7 ± 0.7) × 10–6 S cm–1 at room temperature in the solid state, which can be further improved to (1.5 ± 0.4) × 10–5 S cm–1 with the assistance of solvents. These results, to the best of our knowledge, demonstrate the first MOF that intrinsically combines electronic and sodium ionic conduction, delivering competitive mixed conductivity among reported MIECs, particularly under ambient temperature. The mixed conductivity originates from the intact d–π conjugation for electronic transport and the sulfonate-functionalized pore walls that afford low-barrier Na+ pathways via a relay transport mechanism involving transient bridging coordination between neighboring sulfonate groups. This work highlights embedding ion-transport functionality directly into conductive MOF backbones as an effective route to structurally well-defined, highly porous framework MIECs for electrochemical and iontronic devices.

**Authors:** Sourav Roy, Long Nguyen Minh Le, A. Aich, Punnatorn Boonyoung, Wachiraporn Poomeesap, Ting-Yang Shen, Yi-Pin Chen, Shih-Yin Huang, S. Maikap  
**Venue/category:** ACS Applied Electronic Materials

**DOI:** `10.1021/acsaelm.6c01408`  
**Source(s):** Semantic Scholar  
**Keyword score:** 6  
**Matched:** bioelectronic  

**Abstract**

In the pursuit of ultra-low-power neuromorphic and bioelectronic systems, this work investigates two complementary SiOx-based resistive-switching architectures with engineered top electrodes: a Cu/CuAl structure employing metallic-filament CBRAM and an IrOx/SiOx/W cross-point architecture employing vacancy-mediated switching. Introducing a Cu–Al alloy interfacial layer into a Cu/SiOx/W structure enables ultralow-current operation with an energy consumption of ∼0.03 fJ/bit, endurance exceeding 109 program/erase (P/E) cycles, and a memory window (MW) greater than 2500. Further advancement to a three-dimensional IrOx/SiOx/W cross-point architecture significantly improves device reliability, achieving endurance beyond 1010 P/E cycles at operating currents of 10 and 50 μA while maintaining stable switching for more than 103 cycles at an ultralow current of 200 nA with an MW exceeding 3 × 104. Beyond memory functionality, the porous IrOx top electrode enables label-free biomarker sensing through its stable redox states and super-Nernstian pH response. TEM, HRTEM, and FESEM analyses reveal a porous nanostructured IrOx architecture that facilitates efficient oxygen exchange, proton transport, and redox activity. The resulting platform achieves ultrasensitive sarcosine detection with a limit of detection as low as ∼10 fM. To examine the feasibility of automated interpretation of sensing signals, current–time responses were analyzed using a Convolutional Spiking Neural Network (CSNN). The model achieved an overall classification accuracy of 95.8%, together with a sensitivity of 96.02% and specificity of 100%. These results demonstrate that complementary top-electrode-engineered SiOx architectures can provide ultralow-power memory operation, while the IrOx/SiOx/W cross-point platform further enables ultrasensitive biomarker detection and AI-assisted signal analysis, highlighting their potential for future bioelectronic and neuromorphic systems.

### 409. [Decoding Cell–Cell Interactions in Neurovascular Unit Co-Cultures Through Molecular Vibrational Spectroscopy and Biology](https://www.semanticscholar.org/paper/b700805e6280f0c75b1f9aa9cf944a7b19557727)

**DOI:** `10.3390/cells15201873`  
**Source(s):** Semantic Scholar  
**Keyword score:** 6  
**Matched:** fluorescence imaging  

**Abstract**

The blood–brain barrier (BBB) is a coordinated multicellular interface in which intercellular communication shapes responses to injury and metabolic stress. However, the contributions of individual cell types to collective stress adaptation remain poorly understood. Here, we examined molecular and metabolic responses of 2D neurovascular unit (NVU) co-culture models comprising endothelial cells, astrocytes, and pericytes exposed to hypoxia or sublethal HgCl2 toxicity. An integrated platform combining gene and protein expression profiling, label-free vibrational spectroscopic imaging, and machine-learning-based analysis was used to assess stress-induced changes in cell-cycle regulation and metabolism across mono- and co-culture configurations. Hypoxia broadly suppressed G1/S and G2/M regulators, inducing cell-cycle arrest with limited cell death, consistent with an adaptive response. HgCl2 caused heterogeneous, model-dependent changes in viability and proliferative capacity. Spectroscopic phenotyping revealed that the triple co-culture exhibited spectral features resembling those of pericyte monocultures, although the contributions of cell–cell interactions and differences in culture conditions could not be distinguished. Fluorescence imaging further revealed mitochondria-associated signals at cell–cell contact sites, suggesting a possible association of mitochondrial material with regions of close cellular interaction. The presence of pericytes was associated with a more stress-resistant cellular phenotype in the investigated co-culture configurations. The developed spectroscopic–molecular platform provides a label-free framework for characterizing cellular communication and stress responses in multicellular NVU co-culture models.

### 410. [Synergistic nanotheranostic platform for fluorescence/magnetic resonance bimodal imaging-guided near infrared photoimmuno-chemodynamic thermal targeting therapy](https://www.semanticscholar.org/paper/268cf79a5b009c96952f0e61a9afebe202a1610f)

**Authors:** Kohei Matsuoka, Mizuki Yamada, Ryu Okada, Mibuko Shimada, M. Yoshida, Rika Fukushima, Kanna Umeda, Ayano Ohashi, C. Oka, Ayako Kato, Misae Shimizu, Chiaki Koike et al.

**Abstract**

Near-infrared (NIR) photoimmunotherapy (PIT) is a promising cancer treatment strategy involving antibody–photoabsorber conjugates. However, the imaging depth and photobleaching susceptibility of the IR700 fluorescence used for treatment monitoring are limited, and antigen heterogeneity within tumors compromises the efficacy of strictly antigen-restricted therapies. We developed a synergistic nanotheranostic platform that integrates NIR-PIT, photothermal therapy, and chemodynamic therapy (CDT) into a single MRI-visible agent (NIR-PICT3). To achieve NIR-PICT3, conjugation of mAb–IRDye700DX (mAb-IR700) to dextran-coated superparamagnetic iron oxide nanoparticles (nanomag-D-spio) yielded nanomag-D-spio-mAb-IR700, enabling dual imaging (IR700 fluorescence/MRI) and targeted cytotoxicity upon 690-nm irradiation. The conjugate retained antigen selectivity, exhibited activatable NIR fluorescence after cellular uptake, generated negative T1- and T2-weighted MRI contrast, and demonstrated robust, cycle-enhanced photothermal performance. Iron-mediated redox catalysis promoted ·OH generation, supporting CDT. In vivo, systemic administration led to marked tumor accumulation, as confirmed by multimodal analyses. Repeated light dosing exploited the NIR-PIT–induced super-enhanced permeability and retention (SUPR) effect, enhancing nanoparticle retention, tumor heating, and therapeutic efficacy. In EGFR+ xenografts, NIR-PICT3 achieved superior tumor regression and survival while inducing photothermal bystander effects in antigen-heterogeneous models. These results establish a modular, image-guided strategy for platform therapy to overcome monitoring depth limitations and antigen heterogeneity in solid tumor NIR-PIT.

### 411. [H 2 O 2 ‐Stimulus‐Responsive NIR‐II Imaging Employing an Organic Radical as Resonance Energy Transfer Acceptor for High‐Security Information Encryption](https://www.semanticscholar.org/paper/decbbf8ba41b94e2beb6b1e79b2c27843765a33b)

**Abstract**

Optical information encryption technology has emerged as a crucial strategy for ensuring data security. In particular, the integration of second near‐infrared (NIR‐II, 1000–1700 nm) fluorescence imaging and chemical stimulus‐triggered decryption presents a promising approach for constructing high‐security optical encryption systems. Nevertheless, no related encryption materials have been reported due to the absence of efficient strategies for modulating NIR‐II fluorescence. To overcome these limitations, an organic radical composite MPTCar 2+ ‐MPTCar •+ ‐CB[8] was rationally designed to modulate the NIR‐II fluorescence of NaYF 4 :3%Nd rare‐earth nanoparticles (RENPs) through resonance energy transfer (RET), facilitating the fabrication of the high‐security encryption material named RENPs‐MPT +• . This material only generated a fluorescence signal under specific NIR‐II imaging conditions after the H 2 O 2 ‐triggered conversion of the radical back to the parent compound. These dual‐security mechanisms endowed the encryption material with a high‐level security. Moreover, the combination of this material with permanently fluorescent RENPs‐MPT 2+ allowed for the introduction of deceptive pseudo‐information to further enhance encryption security, offering an innovative model for the development of high‐performance advanced optical encryption technologies.

### 412. [ESIPT-induced selective tryptophan detection: a multifunctional probe for biomacromolecule binding with theoretical and biocompatibility studies.](https://www.semanticscholar.org/paper/9b405042b9ec7f457c7fe846363a54a2cc3b7283)

**Authors:** Sylvina Aquinus, Avijit Kumar Das, Hazeena Shinziya, Gouri Karan, S. Choudhury  
**Venue/category:** In Analysis  
**Date:** 2026-10-14  
**DOI:** `10.1039/d6an00847j`  
**Source(s):** Semantic Scholar  
**Keyword score:** 6  
**Matched:** fluorescence imaging  

**Abstract**

A coumarin-based fluorescent ligand, 4-hydroxy-2-oxo-2H-chromene-3-carbaldehyde (HCC), was synthesized and evaluated for the selective detection of tryptophan (Trp) among the nine essential amino acids. The probe exhibited remarkable selectivity toward Trp through an excited-state intramolecular proton transfer (ESIPT) mechanism facilitated by intramolecular hydrogen transfer. Upon interaction with Trp, HCC forms an HCC-Trp complex (HCP), which undergoes a transient tautomeric transformation between enol (E) and keto (K) forms via an excited-state intramolecular proton transfer (ESIPT) process. This results in an approximately 11-fold enhancement in fluorescence intensity at 385 nm, accompanied by a bathochromic shift in both the absorption and emission spectra. The probe HCC exhibited a low detection limit of 1.53 μM and a binding constant of 1.6 × 104 M-1 towards Trp. The sensing behaviour and binding mechanism were systematically confirmed using UV-Vis and fluorescence spectroscopy, 1H NMR, mass spectrometry, Job's plot analysis, and density functional theory (DFT) calculations. Additionally, HCC exhibited notable binding affinity toward biomacromolecules such as calf thymus DNA (ct-DNA), bovine serum albumin (BSA), and pig liver esterase (PLE), supported by spectroscopic studies and molecular docking analysis. The binding constant for ct-DNA, BSA and PLE was found to be 5.7 × 104 M-1, 5.6 × 104 M-1 and 1.19 × 105 M-1, respectively. Fluorescence imaging experiments further confirmed the applicability of HCC for detecting Trp in biological samples along with in vitro anticancer studies. Thus, this study demonstrates that a simple coumarin-based small-molecule probe can selectively recognize tryptophan through an ESIPT-driven mechanism. The probe HCC exhibits high sensitivity, strong binding affinity, and compatibility with biological systems, enabling fluorescence-based detection and imaging of Trp. The findings highlight the potential of HCC as a multifunctional probe for biochemical sensing and provide a promising platform for developing small-molecule fluorophores for amino acid recognition.

**Abstract**

Colorectal cancer (CRC) is among the leading causes of cancer-related deaths globally. While surgical resection remains the primary treatment, the inaccurate identification of tumor margins and the presence of undetectable occult metastases pose significant clinical challenges. Near-infrared (NIR) fluorescence guided surgery (FGS) presents a potential solution to these issues; however, currently available clinical probes suffer from insufficient targeting specificity and limited overall imaging performance. This study introduces a novel NIR fluorescent probe, E-PEG4-S0456, designed to target fibroblast growth factor receptor (FGFR) for precise imaging of CRC. This probe demonstrates excellent photophysical properties and a strong binding affinity to FGFR, allowing for long-term, high-contrast imaging of both primary and metastatic CRC lesions across various animal models, achieving high tumor-to-background ratios and favorable biosafety profiles. Given these advantages, E-PEG4-S0456 has been validated as a promising tool with the potential to advance NIR−FGS technology and enhance the precision of diagnosis and treatment for CRC.

**Abstract**

Chemotherapy for advanced breast cancer may promote metastatic progression through inflammatory responses. To address this challenge, we developed carrier-free Cur/DTX@ICG nanoparticles via molecular co-assembly, achieving a high drug-loading capacity of 66.7%. This theranostic platform integrates docetaxel, curcumin, and indocyanine green for chemotherapy, anti-inflammatory chemosensitization, and fluorescence imaging. Cur/DTX@ICG effectively inhibited triple-negative breast cancer (TNBC) cell proliferation and migration in vitro. In vivo, it suppressed subcutaneous tumor growth by 74.5% and achieved 77.1% inhibition of postoperative lung metastasis while prolonging survival. Mechanistically, Cur/DTX@ICG attenuated chemotherapy-associated inflammatory responses by reducing serum TNF-α, IL-6, and IL-1β levels, suppressing nuclear factor-κB (NF-κB) activation, and partially reversing epithelial-mesenchymal transition (EMT) progression. These findings suggest that Cur/DTX@ICG represents a promising carrier-free strategy for suppressing inflammation-associated metastatic progression in TNBC.

**Abstract**

Fluorescence imaging holds theoretical sensitivity for single-cell molecular diagnosis, yet practical performance is limited by residual probe-intrinsic fluorescence arising from incomplete control of intramolecular electron transfer within the probe scaffold. Inspired by natural photosynthesis, where spatially organized pigments enable quantum-coherent excitation transport and precise intermolecular electron transfer, we develop a bioinspired quantum chemistry-guided engineering (BQCE) strategy that programs intermolecular electron transfer to construct molecular assembly based background-eliminating activatable molecule probes (BEAMs). Guided by BQCE, the peroxynitrite-responsive hemicyanine-based BEAM1, despite the intrinsically difficult-to-quench nature of this fluorophore scaffold, achieves near-zero background fluorescence, 103.5-fold activation, and an unprecedented detection limit of 1.3 nM. BEAM1 resolves basal ONOO– fluxes at single-cell resolution, enabling single-cell molecular diagnosis that distinguishes malignant from normal cells based solely on endogenous redox signatures. In vivo, BEAM1 enables detection of lymph nodes containing sparsely distributed metastatic cells, with subsequent ex vivo histological analysis identifying metastatic deposits as small as ∼ 35 μm, extending fluorescence imaging into the regime of single-cell level early cancer detection.

**Abstract**

BACKGROUND The peeled stem of Syringa pinnatifolia Hemsl. (SP) has traditionally been used to treat ischemic heart diseases in Inner Mongolia region, China. Through a modified bioactivity-guided fractionation, our group previously identified I3 as the equivalent and pharmacological fraction of total SP extract; however, its specific constituents and mechanisms remain unknown. PURPOSE The study aims to identify the anti-acute myocardial ischemia (AMI) constituents of SP and elucidate the molecular target and mechanisms of the pharmacological bioactive constituents. METHODS The fraction I3 was separated by using ODS and silica gel column chromatography, and five monoeoxylignans (1-5) were isolated. The anti-AMI effects of fractions and a single molecule pinnatifolin (3) were evaluated in mice. Ferroptosis and mitochondrial functions were assessed by biochemical assays, fluorescence imaging, and western blotting techniques. Potential molecular targets were screened by proteomic profiling and further validated by molecular docking, cellular thermal shift assay, and SPR. RESULTS Fraction P retained the equivalent anti-AMI effect comparable to that of I3. P suppressed ferroptosis, and preserved mitochondrial function, accompanied by activation of the AMPK/PGC-1α pathway. P2 was demonstrated as a bioactive subfraction, from which five monoeoxylignans were isolated. Among them, pinnatifolin improved cardiac function and attenuated ferroptotic injury. GPX4 was proved as a potential molecular target underlying the cardioprotective effect of pinnatifolin. CONCLUSIONS A major lignan pinnatifolin exerts anti-AMI effects equivalent to its parent fraction P2 and P of SP.P exerts dual cardioprotective effects via AMPK/PGC-1α activation and ferroptosis suppression, while pinnatilolin acts through GPX4-dependent ferroptosis inhibition.

### 417. [Finishing Touch via Metal Coordination: A Photoactivatable Organic Semiconducting Prodrug for Triply Synergistic Amplified Cancer Phototheranostics](https://www.semanticscholar.org/paper/0919438067aaa0884bff80160be73bea9322d3aa)

**Abstract**

Photothermal therapy (PTT), photodynamic therapy (PDT), and NIR‑II fluorescence imaging are each compromised by tumor hypoxia, thermotolerance, and insufficient contrast, respectively, limiting the efficacy of integrated phototheranostics. To address these challenges, we developed a multi‑amplification theranostic nanoplatform (ReCO‐TPP NPs) that initially exhibits weak NIR‐II fluorescence, type I PDT, and PTT, yet undergoes substantial self‐amplification upon light irradiation. A rhenium(I) carbonyl complex was covalently conjugated to a D‐π‐A‐π‐D fluorophore (DTP), and the heavy‐metal center partially quenches the NIR‐II emission and suppresses type I ROS generation. Upon tumor accumulation and 808 nm laser irradiation, the limited initial ROS cleave the rhenium‐carbonyl bond, releasing CO and regenerating the free DTP fluorophore. This activation dramatically amplifies NIR‐II brightness and type I photodynamic activity, while maintaining excellent photothermal performance. The liberated CO suppresses heat shock protein 70, dissipates mitochondrial membrane potential, and inhibits ATP synthesis, thereby sensitizing tumor cells to PTT and establishing a self‐reinforcing ROS‐CO‐ROS cycle. Mitochondrial targeting via triphenylphosphonium further intensifies cellular damage. In 4T1 tumor‐bearing mice, ReCO‐TPP NPs enable deep‐tissue NIR‐II imaging with post‐irradiation brightness enhancement and produce potent synergistic antitumor efficacy. This molecularly engineered “weak‐to‐strong” theranostic strategy offers a promising paradigm for precisely amplified cancer phototheranostics.

### 418. [Fluorescence-illuminated diffraction tomography using explicit neural fields](https://www.semanticscholar.org/paper/2abc7e6aab13810c4628e10e4f8894f6f6167ef3)

**Authors:** Ren-Zhi He, Yu-Cheng Li, Jun-Jie Chen, Truc M. Dinh, Charlotte R. Stevens, A. Gilchrist, Yi Xue  
**Venue/category:** Nature Communications  
**Date:** 2026-10-10  
**DOI:** `10.1038/s41467-026-78326-x`  
**Source(s):** Semantic Scholar  
**Keyword score:** 6  
**Matched:** fluorescence imaging  

**Abstract**

Multimodal fluorescence and phase imaging provides complementary information about biological samples. However, existing simultaneous fluorescence-phase techniques primarily operate in transmission mode and are limited to thin specimens, restricting their use in thick tissues and in vivo applications. Although multiphoton microscopy enables deep-tissue fluorescence imaging, combining it with three-dimensional refractive-index (RI) imaging remains challenging because few methods can reconstruct the RI of bulky, label-free tissues in reflection mode at subcellular resolution. Here, we introduce fluorescence-illuminated diffraction tomography (FDT), which reconstructs the 3D RI of label-free and fluorescence-labeled objects from diffracted fluorescence images acquired in reflection mode under two-photon excitation. FDT uses the transport of intensity equation and a self-supervised neural network based on explicit neural fields. Compared with state-of-the-art implicit neural fields, this approach improves computational speed, reconstruction accuracy, and interpretability. We demonstrate FDT in two experimental regimes: reconstructing the 3D RI of label-free tissues up to 300 μ m thick placed on a fluorescent substrate, and reconstructing the 3D RI of fluorescence-labeled, 3D-cultured cells without transmission-side access using fluorescence emitted by the cells themselves. FDT establishes a foundation for accessible reflection-mode multimodal fluorescence-phase imaging in complex biological systems.

Pectic acid-based polyacrylate derivatives decorated with Co3O4 NPs formed a larger nanocomposite aggregate with an average size of approximately 67 nm, as observed by TEM. Electrochemical techniques including cyclic voltammetry (CV) and electrochemical impedance spectroscopy (EIS) confirmed the superior charge-transfer capability and enlarged electrochemically active surface area of the nanocomposite-modified electrode compared with those of the bare electrode. Under the optimized experimental conditions, the fabricated sensor exhibited a rapid amperometric response, excellent sensitivity, a broad linear working range from 0.01 to 800 µM, an ultralow limit of detection (LOD) of 3 nM, a limit of quantification (LOQ) of 9 nM and high selectivity toward H2O2 in the presence of common interfering species. The practical applicability of the sensing platform was successfully validated by determining H2O2 in commercial apple juice samples using the standard addition method, providing satisfactory recoveries and low relative standard deviations. The combination of a simple fabrication procedure, low-cost materials, and outstanding electrochemical performance makes the PcA-g-poly(Am-co-AcA)/Co3O4 nanocomposite a promising candidate for routine food safety monitoring and the development of advanced polymer-based nanozyme sensors, alongside its electroactivity exploitation in catalytic systems and other advanced bioelectronic technologies.

### 421. [Molecular Characterization of Soritesidine Reveals a Novel Marine Sponge DNase I-like Toxin](https://www.semanticscholar.org/paper/c8115d2152689e49680e935b70e5cb4ec70aea77)

**Abstract**

Soritesidine (SOR) is a potent proteinaceous toxin from the marine sponge Spongosorites sp. that is toxic to mammalian cells, brine shrimp larvae, sea hare embryos, and mice. Although SOR exhibits divalent metal ion-dependent DNase activity, its molecular identity and mechanism of action remained unknown. We determined the amino acid sequence of SOR and investigated its cellular mode of action. Degenerate PCR, 3′-RACE, inverse PCR, and nested PCR identified an open reading frame encoding a 947-amino-acid acidic protein. The deduced sequence showed no substantial similarity to known proteins except for approximately 30% sequence identity to a hypothetical protein from the marine α-proteobacterium Euryhalocaulis caribicus. Analysis of the AlphaFold 3-predicted N-terminal domain using Foldseek and structural superposition revealed a DNase I-like fold with a catalytic architecture similar to that of vertebrate DNase I. A recombinant N-terminal fragment (residues 1–310, N-SOR) exhibited DNA cleavage activity, supporting the assignment of this region as a DNase I-like endonuclease domain. The recombinant fragment lacked detectable cytotoxicity, suggesting that regions outside the N-terminal DNase I-like domain contribute to the cytotoxicity of SOR. Fluorescence imaging showed that SOR entered cells and accumulated in the nucleus, whereas its cellular uptake was attenuated at 4 °C, suggesting the involvement of an energy-dependent internalization pathway. Immunostaining revealed increased γH2AX signals in the nuclei of SOR-treated cells, indicating activation of a DNA damage response. Together, these findings identify SOR as an unusual multidomain protein toxin containing a functional DNase I-like domain and provide a molecular basis for further investigation of its cytotoxic mechanism.

### 422. [Exciton-driven optics and molecular recognition in carbon nanotube nanosensors: from single-molecule detection to living plant diagnostics](https://www.semanticscholar.org/paper/0759c0322530ef77a0b5a62413fa8f8b802fb3ed)

**Authors:** M. Strano  
**Venue/category:** Low-Dimensional Materials and Devices 2026  
**Date:** 2026-10-09  
**DOI:** `10.1117/12.3122988`  
**Source(s):** Semantic Scholar  
**Keyword score:** 6  
**Matched:** biointegrated  

**Abstract**

Fluorescent single-walled carbon nanotubes (SWCNTs) have emerged as uniquely powerful optical probes for biosensing owing to their stable near-infrared emission, excitonic sensitivity to local perturbations, and compatibility with scattering biological media. This plenary will highlight how nanoscale optical phenomena—specifically one-dimensional exciton transport and quenching—enable single-molecule detection through discrete, stochastic fluorescence modulation events. By engineering the corona phase surrounding SWCNTs, including DNA, polymers, and aptamer-functionalized coatings, molecular recognition can be programmed without perturbing the nanotube’s intrinsic photophysics. These corona phase molecular recognition (CoPhMoRe) strategies produce highly selective and reversible optical responses to analytes ranging from nitric oxide and hydrogen peroxide to proteins and neurotransmitters. Combined with analytical frameworks such as hidden Markov modeling and birth–death kinetics, these optical nanosensors extract quantitative molecular dynamics from fluorescence trajectories, enabling real-time, label-free measurements of single-molecule adsorption, desorption, and cellular efflux processes. This presentation will further describe advances in optical instrumentation and signal processing, including wavelength-induced frequency filtering (WIFF), which enhances signal-to-noise ratios in highly scattering environments and extends nanosensor imaging to centimeter-scale tissue depths. These developments have enabled unprecedented applications in living systems, from mapping reactive oxygen species signaling at single-cell resolution to detecting protein secretion from individual microorganisms in real time. The convergence of near-infrared photonics, nanoscale molecular recognition, and in vivo-compatible sensing culminates in recent breakthroughs in plant biology, where SWCNT-based optical nanosensors non-invasively track hormonal and oxidative signaling dynamics across intact tissues. Applications including real-time imaging of wound-induced hydrogen peroxide waves, multiplexed detection of stress signaling molecules, and direct measurement of plant hormones such as auxin illustrate a new paradigm for precision agriculture. By enabling continuous, spatially resolved biochemical monitoring in crops, these optical nanosensor platforms open pathways toward early stress diagnostics, climate-resilient agriculture, and next-generation biointegrated sensing technologies.
